For $u\in\mathcal U(D)$, let $\theta_u$ be the trace-preserving free-product automorphism which equals $\operatorname{Ad}u$ on $D$ and is the identity on $A_0$. Its Hilbert-space implementation $W_u$ acts by $\theta_u$ on bounded words. In particular, $$W_uL_xW_u^*=L_{\theta_u(x)},\qquad
 W_u\xi=\xi\quad(\xi\in L^2(A_0)).$$

<a id="stmt-lem-free-symmetrization"></a>
<div id="lem:free-symmetrization" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11** (Symmetrization). *In proving Theorem <a href="#thm:free-corner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:free-corner">9</a>, one may suppose in addition that $G$ commutes with $W_u$ for every $u\in\bigcup_j\mathcal U(D_j)$. This replacement preserves the compressed commutator in <a href="#eq:free-corner-conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:free-corner-conclusion">[eq:free-corner-conclusion]</a> exactly, and preserves <a href="#eq:free-corner-hypothesis" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:free-corner-hypothesis">[eq:free-corner-hypothesis]</a> for both $G$ and $G^*$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-free-symmetrization"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-free-symmetrization</summary>

*Proof.* Average the original operator over each compact matrix unitary group: $$G_j=\int_{\mathcal U(D_j)}W_uGW_u^*\,d\mu_j(u).$$ This weak operator integral is well defined. The unitary representation $u\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}%
  \mapsto
  \EndAccSupp{}}W_u$ is strongly continuous on each group, as is seen first on bounded reduced words and then by density. Every conjugate in the integral commutes with $L_D$. It also obeys <a href="#eq:free-corner-hypothesis" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:free-corner-hypothesis">[eq:free-corner-hypothesis]</a>, since $\theta_u$ permutes the $D$-corners of trace $t$. Convexity and weak operator closedness of norm balls preserve these properties under integration and under a weak operator cluster limit $\widetilde G$ as $j\to\infty$. Such a limit exists because $\|G_j\|\leq\|G\|$. For fixed $u\in\mathcal U(D_i)$, all $G_j$ with $j\geq i$ commute with $W_u$, by Haar invariance. Thus $\widetilde G$ has every stated symmetry.

The operators $W_u$ fix $L^2(A_0)$ pointwise and commute with $L_s$. For each conjugate, and therefore for the averages and their limit, $$P_{L^2(A_0)}[W_uGW_u^*,L_s]\big|_{L^2(A_0)}
   =P_{L^2(A_0)}[G,L_s]\big|_{L^2(A_0)}.$$ Finally, $[G^*,L_x]=-[G,L_{x^*}]^*$, so the hypothesis for a self-adjoint corner algebra also gives its adjoint version. The same applies to $\widetilde G$. ◻

</details>

For the remainder of the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:free-corner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:free-corner">9</a>, $G$ denotes the symmetrized operator. At the end we recover the original compressed commutator using Lemma <a href="#lem:free-symmetrization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:free-symmetrization">11</a>.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### The word decomposition

<a id="eq:word-decomposition"></a>
Set $$E=L^2(D),\quad E^\circ=E\ominus\mathbb C1,
 \qquad B=L^2(A_0),\quad B^\circ=B\ominus\mathbb C1,
 \qquad J_k=(B^\circ)^{\otimes k}\otimes B.$$ We use the following ordering of the reduced-word factors: $$\label{eq:word-decomposition}
 L^2(\mathcal L)
   =\bigoplus_{k\geq0}\mathcal H_k,
 \qquad
 \mathcal H_k=E\otimes(E^\circ)^{\otimes k}\otimes J_k.$$ The tensor $$d_0\otimes d_1\otimes\cdots\otimes d_k
       \otimes(a_1\otimes\cdots\otimes a_k\otimes z)$$ represents the word $d_0a_1d_1\cdots a_kd_kz$. Initially the letters may be taken bounded, with $d_i\in D\ominus\mathbb C1$ and $a_i\in A_0\ominus\mathbb C1$ for $i\geq1$, and unrestricted $d_0\in D$, $z\in A_0$. Expanding the endpoints into scalar and centered parts produces all the usual reduced-word spaces exactly once. This proves both orthogonality and completeness in <a href="#eq:word-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:word-decomposition">[eq:word-decomposition]</a>; the tensor notation then extends to arbitrary $L^2$-letters by the corresponding isometries. On each level, $L_D$ acts on the first factor, while $W_u$ conjugates all $k+1$ factors coming from $D$ and fixes $J_k$.

We will show that $G$ preserves each word level $\mathcal H_k$ and, on tensors with a repeated centered $D$ letter, changes only the $J_k$ factor. Scalar slices against fixed vectors in the $J_k$ and $J_l$ factors reduce these assertions to the following intertwiner calculation. Write $C_u\xi=u\xi u^*$ on $E$ and on its invariant subspace $E^\circ$.

<a id="stmt-lem-invariant-intertwiners"></a>
<div id="lem:invariant-intertwiners" class="lemma">

**Lemma 12** (Invariant intertwiners). *Let $k,l\geq0$, and let $$Y:E\otimes(E^\circ)^{\otimes k}
       \longrightarrow E\otimes(E^\circ)^{\otimes l}$$ be bounded. Suppose that $Y$ intertwines the left $D$ actions and that $$Y C_u^{\otimes(k+1)}=C_u^{\otimes(l+1)}Y
 \qquad(u\in\bigcup_j\mathcal U(D_j)).$$ Then $Y=0$ if $k\ne l$. If $k=l$, there are scalars $(a_\sigma)_{\sigma\in S_k}$ such that $$Y=I_E\otimes\sum_{\sigma\in S_k}a_\sigma U_\sigma,
 \qquad
 U_\sigma(x_1\otimes\cdots\otimes x_k)
   =x_{\sigma^{-1}(1)}\otimes\cdots\otimes x_{\sigma^{-1}(k)}.$$ For $k=0$ the sum consists of the scalar multiple of the identity corresponding to the empty permutation.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-invariant-intertwiners"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-invariant-intertwiners</summary>

<a id="eq:coefficient-bound"></a>
*Proof.* For a vector or algebra element $x\in E$, put $x^\circ=x-\tau(x)1$. On unrestricted variables $a,x_1,\ldots,x_k,z_1,\ldots,z_l\in D$, define the complex multilinear form $$F(a,x,z)=
 \left\langle
 Y(1\otimes x_1^\circ\otimes\cdots\otimes x_k^\circ),
 a^*\otimes(z_1^*)^\circ\otimes\cdots\otimes(z_l^*)^\circ
 \right\rangle.$$ It is multilinear with our convention that inner products are linear in the first argument. Fixing the tail vectors and taking their Hilbert-space slice of $Y$ gives an operator $T:E\to E$ commuting with $L_D$. The tracial commutation theorem gives $T=R_b$ for a bounded $b\in D$, with $$\|b\|=\|T\|
  \leq\|Y\|\prod_i\|x_i^\circ\|_2
                 \prod_j\|(z_j^*)^\circ\|_2.$$ Since $F(a,x,z)=\tau(ab)$, this yields the endpoint estimate $$\label{eq:coefficient-bound}
 |F(a,x,z)|\leq
 \|Y\|\,\|a\|_1
       \prod_{i=1}^k\|x_i\|_2
       \prod_{j=1}^l\|z_j\|_2.$$ The same slice also identifies coefficients with arbitrary bounded first endpoints: replacing $1,a^*$ by $d,c$ replaces $a$ by $c^*d$, because $\langle db,c\rangle=\tau(c^*db)$.

*Finite matrix invariants and stable coefficients.* Put $p=1+k+l$ and restrict all variables to a stage $D_j\cong M_m(\mathbb C)$. The intertwining assumption says that $F$ is invariant under simultaneous unitary conjugation. To see precisely the finite-dimensional consequence, label its variables $v_0,\ldots,v_{p-1}$. Every multilinear form on $M_m^p$ has a unique expression $$\operatorname{Tr}\bigl(A(v_0\otimes\cdots\otimes v_{p-1})\bigr)
 \qquad
 (A\in\operatorname{End}((\mathbb C^m)^{\otimes p})),$$ by the nondegenerate trace pairing. Conjugation invariance is equivalent to $A$ commuting with every $u^{\otimes p}$. Finite-dimensional Schur–Weyl duality identifies this commutant with the span of the tensor permutation operators; they are linearly independent when $m\geq p$ (Grinko and Ozols 2024, Theorem 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[GrinkoOzols2024](#ref-GrinkoOzols2024)]. Taking their trace pairings gives the cycle-trace forms $$\Phi_\pi(v_0,\ldots,v_{p-1})
   =\prod_{(i_1\,\cdots\,i_r)\text{ a cycle of }\pi}
       \tau(v_{i_1}\cdots v_{i_r}),
 \qquad \pi\in S_p.$$ Here cycles follow the successor convention of the displayed product; reversing the convention only reindexes the permutations. The normalization from $\operatorname{Tr}$ to $\tau$ multiplies each form by a nonzero scalar, so these forms are also independent for $m\geq p$. Thus $$F\big|_{D_j^p}=\sum_{\pi\in S_p}\lambda_{\pi,j}\Phi_\pi.$$ This identity is on *unrestricted* matrix variables: the centering occurs inside the definition of $F$. Under a unital inclusion of full matrix stages, normalized traces agree, and hence every $\Phi_\pi$ restricts to the identically defined form on the smaller stage. Uniqueness on any smaller stage of size at least $p$ proves that $\lambda_{\pi,j}$ is independent of $j$ from that point onward. Denote it by $\lambda_\pi$.

*Isolating coefficients before restricting to centered tails.* The invariant-theory step has expressed every coefficient through finitely many cycle-trace forms with coefficients fixed across matrix stages. We now use the endpoint bound to remove long cycles, then Hilbert-space boundedness to remove pairs within one side. Fix $\pi\in S_p$. Choose distinct matrix indices for the slots and put $v_i=e_{i,\pi(i)}$. In a nonzero product of these matrix units, the variable in slot $i$ must be followed by the variable whose initial index is $\pi(i)$. Closing each product under its trace therefore forces exactly the cycles of $\pi$. Consequently $$\Phi_\rho(v_0,\ldots,v_{p-1})
  =\begin{cases}
      m^{-c(\pi)},&\rho=\pi,\\
      0,&\rho\ne\pi,
    \end{cases}$$ where $c(\pi)$ is the number of cycles. Every chosen matrix unit has $L^1$ norm $m^{-1}$ and $L^2$ norm $m^{-1/2}$, including diagonal units. Applying <a href="#eq:coefficient-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:coefficient-bound">[eq:coefficient-bound]</a> gives $$|\lambda_\pi|m^{-c(\pi)}
       \leq\|Y\|m^{-1-(k+l)/2}.$$ The coefficient is fixed while the stage size tends to infinity. It follows that $$\lambda_\pi=0\qquad\text{if}\qquad
 c(\pi)<1+\frac{k+l}{2}.$$

Now restrict the $k+l$ tail variables to centered matrices. Any form with a singleton tail cycle vanishes. For a permutation with no such singleton, let $r$ be the length of the cycle containing the distinguished variable $a=v_0$. Its number of cycles is at most $$1+\frac{k+l-(r-1)}2.$$ If $r>1$, this is below the required threshold. If $r=1$, the threshold is attained only when every tail cycle has length two. Thus the only remaining terms have $a$ as a singleton and pair all tail slots. On centered tails, $$F(a,x,z)=\tau(a)
       \sum_{\mathcal P}\lambda_{\mathcal P}
          \prod_{\{i,j\}\in\mathcal P}\tau(v_i v_j),$$ where $\mathcal P$ ranges over pairings of the tail slots. There are no terms when the number of tail slots is odd.

Using $a=c^*d$ gives the corresponding simple-tensor coefficients with first endpoints $d,c$ in the union of the matrix stages. These formulas extend to arbitrary endpoints in $E$ and arbitrary centered $L^2$ tail letters. Indeed, that union is $L^2$ dense, the left side is continuous in its individual $L^2$ letters, and each factor on the right is continuous by $|\tau(uv)|\leq\|u\|_2\|v\|_2$. At this point the assertion is continuity of coefficients on simple tensors, not boundedness of each pairing on the completed tensor product.

*Excluding pairings within one side.* Choose an infinite orthonormal family $(h_n)$ of self-adjoint centered vectors in $E$. For example, normalized real trigonometric functions of a Haar unitary give such a family. Then $\tau(h_nh_m)=\delta_{nm}$. Fix a pairing $\mathcal P$, with $r_s$ pairs entirely in the source slots and $r_t$ entirely in the test slots. Assign a disjoint infinite pool of the $h_n$ to each pair. For each source–test pair, use one fixed member of its pool in both slots. For each source–source pair, sum the source tensor over $M$ members of its pool, placing the same member in its two slots, independently for the different pairs. Form the test tensor analogously for the test–test pairs. Take both first endpoints to be $1$.

In any term of the expanded coefficient every label occurs exactly twice, in the positions assigned by $\mathcal P$, and different pairs have different labels. Hence all other pairings contribute zero. The resulting coefficient is exactly $\lambda_{\mathcal P}M^{r_s+r_t}$. The source summands are orthonormal, as are the test summands, so the product of the two tensor norms is $M^{(r_s+r_t)/2}$. Boundedness of $Y$ gives $$|\lambda_{\mathcal P}|M^{r_s+r_t}
       \leq\|Y\|M^{(r_s+r_t)/2}.$$ Letting $M\to\infty$ proves $\lambda_{\mathcal P}=0$ whenever $r_s+r_t>0$. The argument also applies when one side has no tail slots.

All surviving pairs therefore join a source slot to a test slot, which requires $k=l$. If these numbers differ, all simple-tensor coefficients vanish and $Y=0$. Otherwise these pairings are indexed by bijections $\sigma\in S_k$, and the coefficients are $$\tau(c^*d)\sum_{\sigma\in S_k}a_\sigma
       \prod_{i=1}^k\tau(x_i z_{\sigma(i)}).$$ Since $\tau(x_i z_j)=\langle x_i,z_j^*\rangle$, these are precisely the coefficients of $I_E\otimes\sum_\sigma a_\sigma U_\sigma$. Equality against all elementary test tensors, and their density, proves the asserted operator identity. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-word-structure"></a>
<div id="lem:word-structure" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:repeated-letter"></a>
**Lemma 13** (Word structure). *The symmetrized operator $G$ preserves every level $\mathcal H_k$ in <a href="#eq:word-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:word-decomposition">[eq:word-decomposition]</a>. There are operators $B_k\in\mathcal B(J_k)$, with $\|B_k\|\leq\|G\|$, such that, for all $d_0\in E$, $x\in E^\circ$, and $\alpha\in J_k$, $$\label{eq:repeated-letter}
 \begin{aligned}
 G(d_0\otimes x^{\otimes k}\otimes\alpha)
     &=d_0\otimes x^{\otimes k}\otimes B_k\alpha,\\
 G^*(d_0\otimes x^{\otimes k}\otimes\alpha)
     &=d_0\otimes x^{\otimes k}\otimes B_k^*\alpha.
 \end{aligned}$$ These are identities in the full space $L^2(\mathcal L)$. The operators $B_k$ are independent of the repeated letter $x$; in particular, $x$ may be any complex centered $L^2$ vector.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-word-structure"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-word-structure</summary>

*Proof.* Let $P_k$ be the level projection. It commutes with $L_D$ and all $W_u$. For $\alpha\in J_k$ and $\beta\in J_l$, take the scalar slice in these factors of $P_lGP_k$ to obtain an operator $$Y_{\alpha,\beta}:
 E\otimes(E^\circ)^{\otimes k}
   \longrightarrow E\otimes(E^\circ)^{\otimes l},
 \qquad
 \|Y_{\alpha,\beta}\|\leq\|G\|\|\alpha\|\|\beta\|.$$ It satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#lem:invariant-intertwiners" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:invariant-intertwiners">12</a>. For $k\ne l$ every such slice vanishes, so $P_lGP_k=0$. Thus the levels reduce $G$.

Fix a unit vector $h\in E^\circ$ and let $V_k:J_k\to\mathcal H_k$ be the isometry $V_k\alpha=1\otimes h^{\otimes k}\otimes\alpha$. Define $B_k=V_k^*GV_k$. On an equal-level scalar slice, the lemma gives $$Y_{\alpha,\beta}
    =I_E\otimes\sum_{\sigma\in S_k}
                        a_\sigma(\alpha,\beta)U_\sigma,
 \qquad
 \langle B_k\alpha,\beta\rangle
       =\sum_{\sigma\in S_k}a_\sigma(\alpha,\beta).$$ Every $U_\sigma$ fixes $x^{\otimes k}$ for every $x\in E^\circ$. Consequently the last scalar is independent of the unit letter used to define $V_k$. The same slice identity, now tested against arbitrary endpoint and tail vectors and arbitrary $\beta$, gives the first equality in <a href="#eq:repeated-letter" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:repeated-letter">[eq:repeated-letter]</a>. These tests determine the whole output on level $k$, and the other levels have already been excluded. This also proves the assertion for every endpoint $d_0\in E$, with no boundedness requirement on its representative. No self-adjointness of $x$ is used: the relevant cross contraction is $\tau(xz)=\langle x,z^*\rangle$.

The same argument applies to $G^*$. Its induced operator on $J_k$ is $V_k^*G^*V_k=(V_k^*GV_k)^*=B_k^*$, proving the second equality. ◻

</details>

At level zero the lemma says $G|_{\mathcal H_0}=I_E\otimes B_0$. Since $B=L^2(A_0)$ is the subspace $\mathbb C1\otimes B$ there, the quantity to be estimated in Theorem <a href="#thm:free-corner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:free-corner">9</a>, after symmetrization, is exactly $$\left\|P_B[G,L_s]\big|_B\right\|
      =\|[B_0,L_s|_B]\|.$$

<a id="sec:propagation"></a>
### Propagation from small corners

Continue with the notation and hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:free-corner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:free-corner">9</a>, and set $H=L^2(\mathcal L)$. After Lemma <a href="#lem:free-symmetrization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:free-symmetrization">11</a>, we may use the symmetrized operator $G$ and the operators $B_k\in\mathcal B(J_k)$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:word-structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:word-structure">13</a>. Our goal is to compare the action of $B_k$ with that of $B_0$ along repeated letters, with an error independent of $k$. We first extract a consequence of the small-corner hypothesis that needs only the exact commutation with $L_D$.

<a id="stmt-lem-full-column"></a>
<div id="lem:full-column" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:full-column"></a>
**Lemma 14** (Full columns from a small corner). *For every projection $r\in\mathcal L$ with $\tau(r)\le t$, there is $b\in\mathcal L$ with $b=rb$ such that $$\big\|(G-R_b)|_{Hr}\big\|\le c_0\epsilon.
  \label{eq:full-column}$$ The same assertion holds for $G^*$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-full-column"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-full-column</summary>

*Proof.* The case $r=0$ is immediate. Fix $r\ne0$ and a projection $e\in D$ with $\tau(e)=t$. The factoriality established in Lemma <a href="#lem:free-factor" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:free-factor">10</a> and comparison of projections give $r'\le e$ and a partial isometry $v\in\mathcal L$ such that $v^*v=r$ and $vv^*=r'$. Right multiplication by $v^*$ is a unitary left $e\mathcal Le$-module map $$eHr\longrightarrow eHr',\qquad \xi\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=27FC}%
  \begingroup
    \let\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}%
  \mapsto
  \EndAccSupp{}}\mapsto
    \let\longrightarrow\longrightarrow
    \longmapsto
  \endgroup
  \EndAccSupp{}}\xi v^*.$$ Indeed its inverse is right multiplication by $v$, and $$\|\xi v^*\|_2^2
    =\tau(v\xi^*\xi v^*)
    =\tau(\xi^*\xi r)=\|\xi\|_2^2.$$ These equalities also hold for $L^2$ vectors by continuity. The vector $r'$ is cyclic for the left action of $e\mathcal Le$ on $eHr'$, since $(e\mathcal Le)r'=e\mathcal Lr'$ is dense in that Hilbert space. Thus $eHr$ is a cyclic domain representation of the unital algebra $e\mathcal Le$.

Since $G$ commutes with $L_e$, its restriction defines a bounded map $G_e:eHr\to eH$. On these domain and codomain representations the map $$\Delta(a)=G_eL_a|_{eHr}-L_aG_e,\qquad a\in e\mathcal Le,$$ is a rectangular derivation of norm at most $\epsilon$, by <a href="#eq:free-corner-hypothesis" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:free-corner-hypothesis">[eq:free-corner-hypothesis]</a>. Lemma <a href="#lem:cyclic-implementation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cyclic-implementation">7</a> therefore supplies an exact intertwiner $T:eHr\to eH$ with $$TL_a|_{eHr}=L_aT\quad(a\in e\mathcal Le),\qquad
  \|G_e-T\|\le c_0\epsilon.$$

The value $t=1/N$ allows an extension that preserves this norm. Choose $v_1,\ldots,v_N\in D$ with $$v_i^*v_i=e,\qquad v_iv_i^*\perp v_jv_j^*\ (i\ne j),\qquad
  \sum_{i=1}^N v_iv_i^*=1.$$ The maps $(\xi_i)\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}%
  \mapsto
  \EndAccSupp{}}\sum_i v_i\xi_i$ identify $(eHr)^N$ with $Hr$ and $(eH)^N$ with $H$ unitarily. Define $$\widetilde T\left(\sum_{i=1}^N v_i\xi_i\right)
      =\sum_{i=1}^N v_iT\xi_i,\qquad \xi_i\in eHr.$$ In these coordinates $\widetilde T$ is $T^{(N)}$, and $G|_{Hr}$ is $G_e^{(N)}$, because $G$ commutes with each $L_{v_i}$. Consequently $$\|G|_{Hr}-\widetilde T\|=\|G_e-T\|\le c_0\epsilon.$$ For $a\in\mathcal L$, its left action has matrix entries $L_{v_i^*av_j}$ in both coordinate systems. Every $v_i^*av_j$ lies in $e\mathcal Le$, so the intertwining identity for $T$ implies $\widetilde T L_a|_{Hr}=L_a\widetilde T$.

Extend $\widetilde T$ by zero on $H(1-r)$. Since $R_r$ commutes with all left multiplications, this extension belongs to $L(\mathcal L)'$ and hence is $R_b$ for a bounded $b\in\mathcal L$, by the tracial commutation theorem. Its vanishing on $H(1-r)$ says $R_b=R_bR_r$, which, with the convention $R_b\xi=\xi b$, gives $b=rb$. This proves <a href="#eq:full-column" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:full-column">[eq:full-column]</a>. Finally $G^*$ commutes with $L_D$ and $[G^*,L_a]=-[G,L_{a^*}]^*$, so all the same hypotheses and estimates hold for $G^*$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-lem-propagation"></a>
<div id="lem:propagation" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:propagation"></a>
**Lemma 15** (Propagation along repeated letters). *For every $k\ge1$ and $v\in B=L^2(A_0)$, $$\big\|B_k(s^{\otimes k}\otimes v)
       -s^{\otimes k}\otimes B_0v\big\|
       \le 2c_0\epsilon\|v\|_2.
  \label{eq:propagation}$$ This also holds with $B_k^*$ and $B_0^*$ in place of $B_k$ and $B_0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-propagation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-propagation</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $k\ge1$ and first take $v\in A_0$; the zero vector is harmless. Choose a nonzero projection $q\in D$ with $a=\tau(q)\le t$. The left support of $qv$ is dominated by $q$. Its right support $r$ is equivalent to its left support by polar decomposition, so $\tau(r)\le a\le t$. Choose $b=rb$ as in Lemma <a href="#lem:full-column" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:full-column">14</a> for this $r$, and put $$y=qvb-qB_0v.$$ The level-zero identity in <a href="#eq:repeated-letter" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:repeated-letter">[eq:repeated-letter]</a> gives $G(qv)=qB_0v$, and the tensor decomposition gives $\|qv\|_2=\sqrt a\,\|v\|_2$. It follows that <a id="eq:propagation-error-vector"></a>$$qy=y,\qquad \|y\|_2\le c_0\epsilon\sqrt a\,\|v\|_2.
  \label{eq:propagation-error-vector}$$ The vector $B_0v$ need only belong to $L^2(A_0)$; every expression here uses bounded left multiplication on this vector.

<a id="eq:propagation-prefix"></a>
Let $z$ be a Haar unitary of $qDq$ for its normalized trace, and set $x_n=a^{-1/2}z^n$ for $n\ge1$. Then $$\tau(x_n)=0,\quad \|x_n\|_2=1,\quad
  x_n^*x_n=x_nx_n^*=a^{-1}q,
  \qquad x_n,x_n^*\rightharpoonup0\ \text{in }L^2(D).$$ The weak convergence follows, for example, from orthonormality of the positive and negative powers. The reduced words $\xi_n=(sx_n)^kv$ have norm $\|v\|_2$ and right support at most $r$, because their last $x_n$ satisfies $x_nq=x_n$. With $A_n=(sx_n)^{k-1}s$, we have $$\xi_nb-(sx_n)^kB_0v=A_nx_ny.$$ Thus <a href="#eq:full-column" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:full-column">[eq:full-column]</a> yields $$\|G\xi_n-(sx_n)^kB_0v\|_2
    \le c_0\epsilon\|v\|_2+\|A_nx_ny\|_2.
  \label{eq:propagation-prefix}$$

<a id="eq:vanishing-tensor-coefficient"></a>
To estimate the second term, write <a href="#eq:word-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:word-decomposition">[eq:word-decomposition]</a> as $H=E\otimes F$, where $$F=\bigoplus_{l\ge0}(E^\circ)^{\otimes l}\otimes J_l,$$ and let $P_{\mathrm{sc}}$ be projection onto $\mathbb C1\otimes F$. This projection keeps every word level and selects the scalar component of its first $D$ factor. On this decomposition left multiplication by $x_n$ acts on $E$. With inner products linear in the first variable, define $\ell_n(\eta)=\langle\eta,x_n^*\rangle$. Then $$P_{\mathrm{sc}}(x_ny)=1\otimes (\ell_n\otimes I_F)y,
  \qquad \|\ell_n\|=1.$$ For each elementary tensor this expression tends to zero in norm, since $x_n^*\rightharpoonup0$. Approximating the fixed vector $y$ by finite tensor sums, and using the common bound $\|\ell_n\|=1$, proves $$\|P_{\mathrm{sc}}(x_ny)\|_2\longrightarrow0.
  \label{eq:vanishing-tensor-coefficient}$$

On $(1-P_{\mathrm{sc}})H$, multiplication by $A_n$ prepends a reduced centered word ending in $s$ to a leading centered $D$ letter. Each prefix letter has $L^2$ norm one, so this restriction is an isometry. On $P_{\mathrm{sc}}H$ we use the bound $\|L_{A_n}\|\le a^{-(k-1)/2}$. Also $qy=y$ gives $$\|x_ny\|_2^2
     =\langle L_{x_n^*x_n}y,y\rangle=a^{-1}\|y\|_2^2.$$ Consequently $$\|A_nx_ny\|_2
   \le \|(1-P_{\mathrm{sc}})x_ny\|_2
        +a^{-(k-1)/2}\|P_{\mathrm{sc}}x_ny\|_2,$$ and <a href="#eq:vanishing-tensor-coefficient" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:vanishing-tensor-coefficient">[eq:vanishing-tensor-coefficient]</a> implies $$\limsup_n\|A_nx_ny\|_2
       \le a^{-1/2}\|y\|_2\le c_0\epsilon\|v\|_2.$$ Here $q,k,v,b,y$ are all fixed before $n$ tends to infinity. The possibly large prefix bound therefore disappears with the vanishing coefficient; a convergence rate uniform in $a$ or $k$ is unnecessary.

By the full-output identity <a href="#eq:repeated-letter" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:repeated-letter">[eq:repeated-letter]</a>, the left side of <a href="#eq:propagation-prefix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:propagation-prefix">[eq:propagation-prefix]</a> equals $$\big\|B_k(s^{\otimes k}\otimes v)
            -s^{\otimes k}\otimes B_0v\big\|$$ for every $n$: its endpoint and repeated $D$ factors all have norm one. Taking the limit superior proves <a href="#eq:propagation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:propagation">[eq:propagation]</a> for bounded $v$. Density of $A_0$ in $B$ and boundedness of the operators extend it to all $v\in B$. Applying the same argument to $G^*$, using the adjoint identities of Lemma <a href="#lem:word-structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:word-structure">13</a>, gives the final assertion. ◻

</details>

The propagation estimate now controls all word levels with the same error. We assemble orthogonal copies of $B$ from these levels; this will allow a single compressed commutator to test $B_0$.

Choose $e\in D$ with $\tau(e)=t$ and $p\in D$ with $e\le p$ and $\tau(p)=1/2$; this is possible since $t\le1/2$. Set <a id="eq:dihedral-parameters"></a>$$d=2p-1,\qquad w=ds,\qquad u=\tau_p(e)=2t,\qquad
  \tau_p=2\tau\big|_{p\mathcal Lp}.
  \label{eq:dihedral-parameters}$$ The elements $d$ and $s$ are free centered symmetries. For $k\ge0$ define $$U_k:B\longrightarrow eH,\qquad
  U_kv=t^{-1/2}ew^{-k}v=t^{-1/2}e(sd)^kv.$$ In <a href="#eq:word-decomposition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:word-decomposition">[eq:word-decomposition]</a>, this is the tensor with endpoint $t^{-1/2}e$, repeated $D$ letter $d$, and last factor $s^{\otimes k}\otimes v$. Hence each $U_k$ is an isometry, its range belongs to level $k$, and these ranges are mutually orthogonal. Put $$\mathcal K=\overline{\bigoplus_{k\ge0}U_kB}\subset eH.$$ Via the $U_k$, identify $\mathcal K$ with $\ell^2(\mathbb N_0)\otimes B$, where $\mathbb N_0=\{0,1,\ldots\}$, and set $J=I\otimes B_0$. Denote the inclusion $\mathcal K\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21AA}%
  \hookrightarrow
  \EndAccSupp{}}H$ by $\iota$.

<a id="stmt-lem-compressed-commutator"></a>
<div id="lem:compressed-commutator" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:compression-bound"></a>
**Lemma 16** (Compression of the commutator). *For every $h\in e\mathcal Le$, $$\big\|[J,\iota^*L_h\iota]\big\|
       \le (1+4c_0)\epsilon\|h\|.
  \label{eq:compression-bound}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-compressed-commutator"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-compressed-commutator</summary>

<a id="eq:two-inclusion-errors"></a>
*Proof.* Lemma <a href="#lem:propagation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:propagation">15</a> and <a href="#eq:repeated-letter" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:repeated-letter">[eq:repeated-letter]</a> give $$\|GU_kv-U_kB_0v\|_2\le2c_0\epsilon\|v\|_2,$$ with zero error for $k=0$. Each error lies in level $k$ by Lemma <a href="#lem:word-structure" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:word-structure">13</a>. Their squared norms therefore add on finite orthogonal sums. Passing to the closure, and repeating the argument for $G^*$, gives the full-codomain estimates $$\|G\iota-\iota J\|\le2c_0\epsilon,
  \qquad \|G^*\iota-\iota J^*\|\le2c_0\epsilon.
  \label{eq:two-inclusion-errors}$$ Write $R=G\iota-\iota J$, $R'=G^*\iota-\iota J^*$, and $T_h=\iota^*L_h\iota$. Taking the adjoint of the second error identity gives $\iota^*G=J\iota^*+(R')^*$. A direct expansion now yields $$\iota^*[G,L_h]\iota
     =[J,T_h]+(R')^*L_h\iota-\iota^*L_hR.$$ Apply <a href="#eq:free-corner-hypothesis" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:free-corner-hypothesis">[eq:free-corner-hypothesis]</a> to the first term and <a href="#eq:two-inclusion-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:two-inclusion-errors">[eq:two-inclusion-errors]</a> to the other two. Their bounds are $\epsilon\|h\|$, $2c_0\epsilon\|h\|$, and $2c_0\epsilon\|h\|$, proving <a href="#eq:compression-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:compression-bound">[eq:compression-bound]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:hankel"></a>
### Free compression and the Hankel test

Retain $e,p,d,w,u$, the isometries $U_k$, and the subspace $\mathcal K$ from the preceding section. We construct $h\in e\mathcal Le$ whose norm is of order $u$ and whose compression to $\mathcal K$ has a Hankel component of the same order, with a uniform lower bound after division by $u$.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### The dihedral corner

Let $\mathcal C=W^*(d,s)$. Freeness of the centered symmetries $d,s$ shows that their alternating reduced words, together with $1$, form an orthonormal word basis of $L^2(\mathcal C)$. These are precisely the group words of the infinite dihedral group. In particular, $$w=ds\text{ is a Haar unitary},\qquad dwd=w^{-1},\qquad p=(1+d)/2.$$ Write $m$ for Haar probability measure on $\mathbb T$.

<a id="stmt-lem-dihedral-corner"></a>
<div id="lem:dihedral-corner" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:dihedral-corner"></a>
**Lemma 17** (The abelian corner and corner freeness). *The algebra $p\mathcal C p$ is identified with the even functions on the circle by $$\begin{gathered}
    p\mathcal C p
      =\{f(w)p:f\in L^\infty(\mathbb T,m),\ f(z)=f(z^{-1})\},\\
    \tau_p(f(w)p)=\int_{\mathbb T}f\,dm.
  \end{gathered}
  \label{eq:dihedral-corner}$$ The map $f\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}%
  \mapsto
  \EndAccSupp{}}f(w)p$ in this formula is a faithful normal unital $*$-isomorphism onto the corner. Moreover, $pDp$ and $p\mathcal C p$ are free with respect to the normalized trace $\tau_p$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-dihedral-corner"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-dihedral-corner</summary>

*Proof.* Every group word has the form $w^l$ or $w^ld$, $l\in\mathbb Z$. Since $dp=p$ and $dw^ld=w^{-l}$, $$pw^ldp=pw^lp=\frac{w^l+w^{-l}}2p.$$ An even function of $w$ commutes with $d$ and hence with $p$. Thus $f\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}%
  \mapsto
  \EndAccSupp{}}f(w)p$ is a normal $*$-homomorphism on the even-function algebra, with unit $p$. The group trace gives $$\tau_p(f(w)p)
      =2\tau(f(w)p)=\tau(f(w))+\tau(f(w)d)=\int_{\mathbb T}f\,dm,$$ initially for trigonometric polynomials and then for bounded functions by $L^2$ approximation. In particular the map is faithful, and its range is a von Neumann algebra. The compressed group-word span is strongly dense in $p\mathcal C p$ and consists of even Laurent polynomials times $p$. This proves <a href="#eq:dihedral-corner" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dihedral-corner">[eq:dihedral-corner]</a> and the isomorphism assertion. It also gives $$\|f(w)p\|=\|f\|_\infty,\qquad
  \|f(w)p\|_{2,\tau_p}=\|f\|_{L^2(m)}.$$

For freeness, let $a_1,\ldots,a_j\in pDp$ be centered for $\tau_p$. Then $\tau(a_i)=0$ and $a_id=a_i$. The centered trigonometric span in $p\mathcal C p$ is generated by the elements $pw^lp$, $l\ge1$. For $l_1,\ldots,l_j\ge1$, absorbing the corner projections and using traciality gives $$\begin{aligned}
 &\tau_p(a_1pw^{l_1}p\cdots a_jpw^{l_j}p)\\
 &\qquad=2\tau(a_1w^{l_1}\cdots a_jw^{l_j})\\
 &\qquad=2\tau\big(a_1s(ds)^{l_1-1}\cdots
                              a_js(ds)^{l_j-1}\big)=0.
\end{aligned}$$ All the displayed $D$ letters are centered, and $s$ is centered and free from the whole algebra $D$, by $\mathcal L=D*A_0$. Thus the last word is a reduced alternating word of trace zero. Fejér means of an even bounded zero-mean function are even zero-mean trigonometric polynomials, are uniformly bounded, and converge in $L^2(m)$. Approximating each corner letter in this manner extends the vanishing to all cyclic alternating products of even length. Products of odd alternating length have their endpoints in the same algebra. Move one endpoint cyclically next to the other, split their product into its centered and scalar parts, and apply the even-length case and induction on the length. The length-one case is centeredness. This proves freeness. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### The expectation and the norm of a free compression

<a id="stmt-lem-free-compression"></a>
<div id="lem:free-compression" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:free-compression"></a>
**Lemma 18** (Free compression). *For $b=b^*\in p\mathcal C p$ with $\tau_p(b)=0$, $$E_{\mathcal C}(ebe)=u^2b,\qquad
  \|ebe\|\le2\sqrt u\,\|b\|_{2,\tau_p}+u\|b\|,
  \qquad u=\tau_p(e).
  \label{eq:free-compression}$$ Here $E_{\mathcal C}$ is the trace-preserving conditional expectation from $\mathcal L$ onto $\mathcal C$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-free-compression"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-free-compression</summary>

*Proof.* Put $\mathcal A=pDp$, $\mathcal Z=p\mathcal C p$, and $a=e-up\in\mathcal A$, so $\tau_p(a)=0$. Since $p\in\mathcal C$, bimodularity shows that $E_{\mathcal C}(ebe)$ belongs to $\mathcal Z$. On $p\mathcal Lp$, its restriction is the trace-preserving expectation onto $\mathcal Z$ for $\tau_p$. Expand $$ebe=u^2b+u(ab+ba)+aba.$$ Freeness gives zero expectation for $ab$ and $ba$. For the last term, test against any $c\in\mathcal Z$ and write $c=c^\circ+\tau_p(c)p$. Freeness and traciality give $$\tau_p(c^\circ aba)=0,
  \qquad \tau_p(aba)=\tau_p(ba^2)
                  =\tau_p(b)\tau_p(a^2)=0.$$ Thus $E_{\mathcal Z}(aba)=0$ also. This proves the expectation formula.

<a id="eq:corner-overlap"></a>
For the norm estimate work in $$H_c=L^2(W^*(\mathcal A,\mathcal Z),\tau_p).$$ This representation is faithful, so its operator norm is the algebra norm. The free-word decomposition, grouping a possible initial $\mathcal A$ letter, takes the form $$H_c=L^2(\mathcal A,\tau_p)\otimes F_0,
  \qquad F_0=\mathbb C\Omega\oplus F_0^\circ,$$ where $F_0^\circ$ is the closed span of reduced words starting in the centered $L^2$ subspace $\mathcal Z^\circ$. Let $Q_0$ be the projection of $F_0$ onto the complement of the vacuum, and let $\Pi$ project in $H_c$ onto words starting in $\mathcal Z^\circ$. Then $$\Pi=|1_{\mathcal A}\rangle\langle1_{\mathcal A}|\otimes Q_0,
  \qquad L_e=\lambda_{\mathcal A}(e)\otimes I_{F_0},$$ where $1_{\mathcal A}=p$ has $L^2(\tau_p)$ norm one. Consequently $$\|L_e\Pi\|=\|\Pi L_e\|\le\|e\|_{2,\tau_p}=\sqrt u.
  \label{eq:corner-overlap}$$

The following three blocks are the standard creation, annihilation, and diagonal decomposition of a centered free-product letter; see Ricard and Xu (2006, sec. 1, Lemmas 2.1–2.3 and Corollary 2.4) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[RicardXu2006](#ref-RicardXu2006)]. We give the bounds needed here directly. Let $T=L_b$ on $H_c$. Because $b$ is centered, on the vacuum and on words starting in $\mathcal A^\circ$ its left action simply prepends the letter $b$. Therefore $$(1-\Pi)T(1-\Pi)=0,\qquad
  \|\Pi T(1-\Pi)\|\le\|b\|_{2,\tau_p}.$$ Self-adjointness gives the same bound for $(1-\Pi)T\Pi$, and $\|\Pi T\Pi\|\le\|b\|$. Decomposing $T$ into these three blocks, using $\|L_e\|\le1$, and applying <a href="#eq:corner-overlap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:corner-overlap">[eq:corner-overlap]</a>, we obtain $$\begin{aligned}
  \|ebe\|=\|L_eTL_e\|
   &\le\|L_e\Pi T(1-\Pi)L_e\|
      +\|L_e(1-\Pi)T\Pi L_e\|
      +\|L_e\Pi T\Pi L_e\|\\
   &\le\sqrt u\,\|b\|_{2,\tau_p}
      +\sqrt u\,\|b\|_{2,\tau_p}+u\|b\|.
\end{aligned}$$ This proves <a href="#eq:free-compression" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:free-compression">[eq:free-compression]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Fourier coefficients of the compressed operator

The preceding lemma gives both the expectation and the upper norm bound for a corner compression. We now compute its matrix entries on $\mathcal K$; their dependence on the sum of the indices will isolate the commutator with $L_s$.

<a id="eq:hankel-compression"></a>
Let $f\in L^\infty(\mathbb T,m)$ be real, even, and of mean zero, and set $$b_f=f(w)p,\qquad h=eb_fe,\qquad
  \gamma_l=\int_{\mathbb T}z^lf(z)\,dm(z)\quad(l\in\mathbb Z).$$ Then $b_f$ meets the hypotheses of Lemma <a href="#lem:free-compression" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:free-compression">18</a>. We claim that, for $j,k\ge0$, $$\begin{aligned}
    U_j^*L_hU_k
      &=L_{t^{-1}E_{A_0}(w^jhw^{-k})}\big|_B\\
      &=u\big(\gamma_{j-k}I_B+\gamma_{j+k+1}L_s|_B\big).
  \end{aligned}
  \label{eq:hankel-compression}$$ For the first equality, test on $v,z\in A_0$. Since $eh=he=h$, $$\begin{aligned}
  \langle L_hU_kv,U_jz\rangle
    &=t^{-1}\tau(z^*w^jhw^{-k}v)\\
    &=\left\langle
        t^{-1}E_{A_0}(w^jhw^{-k})v,z\right\rangle.
\end{aligned}$$ Density then gives the operator identity on $B$.

The reduced-word decomposition shows that on $W^*(D,s)$ the expectation $E_{A_0}$ equals the expectation onto $W^*(s)=\mathbb C1+\mathbb Cs$: every reduced word containing a centered $D$ letter is orthogonal to $L^2(A_0)$. Because $W^*(s)\subset\mathcal C$, this latter expectation factors through $E_{\mathcal C}$. By <a href="#eq:free-compression" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:free-compression">[eq:free-compression]</a> and bimodularity, $$E_{\mathcal C}(w^jhw^{-k})=u^2w^jb_fw^{-k}.$$ Using $dw^{-k}=w^kd$ and $d=ws$, compute $$w^jpw^{-k}
     =\frac{w^{j-k}+w^{j+k}d}{2}
     =\frac{w^{j-k}+w^{j+k+1}s}{2}.$$ The group trace implies $$E_{W^*(s)}(f(w)w^l)=\gamma_l1,\qquad
  E_{W^*(s)}(f(w)w^ls)=\gamma_ls.$$ For instance this follows on Laurent polynomials because only the identity power contributes, and then by $L^2$ approximation. Hence $$t^{-1}E_{A_0}(w^jhw^{-k})
     =\frac{u^2}{2t}
          (\gamma_{j-k}1+\gamma_{j+k+1}s)
     =u(\gamma_{j-k}1+\gamma_{j+k+1}s),$$ which proves <a href="#eq:hankel-compression" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hankel-compression">[eq:hankel-compression]</a>, including its normalization and its Hankel index.

<a id="eq:hankel-commutator"></a>
The matrices $$T_f=(\gamma_{j-k})_{j,k\ge0},\qquad
  H_f=(\gamma_{j+k+1})_{j,k\ge0}$$ define bounded operators on $\ell^2(\mathbb N_0)$ of norm at most $\|f\|_\infty$. To see this directly, decompose $$L^2(\mathbb T,m)=H_-\oplus H_+,\qquad
  H_-=\overline{\operatorname{span}}\{z^{-j}:j\ge0\},\quad
  H_+=\overline{\operatorname{span}}\{z^{k+1}:k\ge0\}.$$ In the indicated orthonormal bases, $T_f$ and $H_f$ are respectively the $H_-,H_-$ and $H_-,H_+$ blocks of multiplication by $f$. Equation <a href="#eq:hankel-compression" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hankel-compression">[eq:hankel-compression]</a>, first on finite coordinate vectors and then by continuity, therefore gives <a id="eq:toeplitz-hankel-form"></a>$$\iota^*L_h\iota
      =u(T_f\otimes I_B+H_f\otimes L_s|_B).
  \label{eq:toeplitz-hankel-form}$$ As $J=I\otimes B_0$, it follows that $$\big\|[J,\iota^*L_h\iota]\big\|
       =u\|H_f\|\,\|[B_0,L_s|_B]\|.
  \label{eq:hankel-commutator}$$ Here we used the operator norm identity $\|A\otimes B\|=\|A\|\|B\|$ for Hilbert-space tensor products.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-free-compression-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-free-compression</summary>

<a id="eq:narrow-arc-norm"></a>
<a id="eq:narrow-arc-hankel"></a>
*Completion of the proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:free-corner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:free-corner">9</a>.* Put $\theta=u/4=1/(2N)$, so $0<\theta\le1/4$, and use angular coordinates $z=e^{i\alpha}$, $-\pi<\alpha\le\pi$, to define $$f(e^{i\alpha})=\mathbf1_{[-\theta,\theta]}(\alpha)-\frac\theta\pi.$$ This function is real, even, and of mean zero. The trace formula in <a href="#eq:dihedral-corner" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dihedral-corner">[eq:dihedral-corner]</a> gives $$\|b_f\|\le1,\qquad
  \|b_f\|_{2,\tau_p}^2
       =\frac\theta\pi\left(1-\frac\theta\pi\right)\le u.$$ Consequently <a href="#eq:free-compression" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:free-compression">[eq:free-compression]</a> gives $$\|h\|=\|eb_fe\|\le3u.
  \label{eq:narrow-arc-norm}$$ For every $l\ge1$ the Fourier coefficient is $$\gamma_l=\frac{\sin(l\theta)}{\pi l}.$$ If $0\le j,k<N$, then $1\le j+k+1\le2N-1$ and $(j+k+1)\theta<1<\pi/2$. The inequality $\sin x\ge2x/\pi$ on $[0,\pi/2]$ therefore gives $$(H_f)_{jk}\ge\frac{2\theta}{\pi^2}.$$ Testing the leading $N$ by $N$ block on its constant unit vector, and using $\theta N=1/2$, proves the uniform bound $$\|H_f\|\ge\frac{2\theta N}{\pi^2}\ge\frac1{2\pi^2}.
  \label{eq:narrow-arc-hankel}$$ Combine <a href="#eq:hankel-commutator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hankel-commutator">[eq:hankel-commutator]</a>, <a href="#eq:compression-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:compression-bound">[eq:compression-bound]</a>, <a href="#eq:narrow-arc-norm" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-arc-norm">[eq:narrow-arc-norm]</a>, and <a href="#eq:narrow-arc-hankel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:narrow-arc-hankel">[eq:narrow-arc-hankel]</a> to obtain $$\frac{u}{2\pi^2}\|[B_0,L_s|_B]\|
       \le3u(1+4c_0)\epsilon.$$ Cancelling $u>0$ yields $$\|[B_0,L_s|_B]\|
      \le6\pi^2(1+4c_0)\epsilon=c_1\epsilon.$$ The level-zero case of <a href="#eq:repeated-letter" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:repeated-letter">[eq:repeated-letter]</a> identifies $G|_B$ with $B_0$, so this is exactly the required compression estimate for the symmetrized operator. Lemma <a href="#lem:free-symmetrization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:free-symmetrization">11</a> preserves the compression of $[G,L_s]$ between vectors of $B$. The same estimate therefore holds for the original operator in Theorem <a href="#thm:free-corner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:free-corner">9</a>, completing its proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:finite-factors"></a>
### Transfer to finite factors

We now transfer Theorem <a href="#thm:free-corner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:free-corner">9</a> to matrix commutators in finite sums of the standard representation of a separable-predual $\mathrm{II}_1$ factor. All traces on matrix amplifications are normalized.

We first average the operator over an irreducible subfactor generated by an increasing sequence of full matrix algebras. After matrix amplification, the ultrapower of the amplified subfactor contains another subfactor generated by increasing full matrix algebras and free from the constant copy of the amplified ambient factor. Extending and compressing the averaged operator then puts it in the setting of Theorem <a href="#thm:free-corner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:free-corner">9</a>. Compression to constant vectors transfers the estimate back to the matrix test, with the fixed error from the initial averaging.

The existence of an irreducible hyperfinite subfactor in a separable-predual $\mathrm{II}_1$ factor is due to Popa (Popa 1981) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[Popa1981](#ref-Popa1981)]; we include a direct proof of the increasing-matrix form used below.

<a id="stmt-lem-irreducible-hyperfinite"></a>
<div id="lem:irreducible-hyperfinite" class="lemma">

**Lemma 19** (An irreducible increasing-matrix subfactor). *Every separable-predual $\mathrm{II}_1$ factor $M$ contains a unital subfactor $R$ with $R'\cap M=\mathbb C1$, generated by an increasing sequence of unital full matrix algebras whose sizes tend to infinity.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-irreducible-hyperfinite"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-irreducible-hyperfinite</summary>

*Proof.* We first give an approximation step in an arbitrary $\mathrm{II}_1$ factor $N$. If $y=y^*\in N$ and $\eta>0$, there is a unital full matrix algebra $F\subset N$, of size at least two, such that $$\lVert E_{F'\cap N}(y)-\tau(y)1\rVert_2<\eta.$$ Indeed, approximate $y$ in $L^2$ by a spectral step $y_0=\sum_{i=1}^k\lambda_i p_i$, where the $p_i$ partition the identity. For a large integer $b$, split off $\lfloor b\tau(p_i)\rfloor$ subprojections of trace $1/b$ in each $p_i$. The remaining projection has trace $$\frac{b-\sum_{i=1}^k\lfloor b\tau(p_i)\rfloor}{b}<\frac{k}{b}.$$ Its numerator is an integer, so it too splits into projections of trace $1/b$. These pieces form a partition of the identity by $b$ equivalent projections and hence extend to matrix units for a unital $F\cong M_b(\mathbb C)$. Define $y_1\in F$ to have value $\lambda_i$ on the pieces taken from $p_i$, and value zero on the remaining pieces. Then $$\lVert y-y_1\rVert_2
 \leq \lVert y-y_0\rVert_2+\lVert y_0\rVert\sqrt{k/b}.$$ The expectation of $y_1$ onto $F'\cap N$ is $\tau(y_1)1$. Contractivity of this expectation on $L^2$, and $\lvert\tau(y-y_1)\rvert\leq\lVert y-y_1\rVert_2$, give the required estimate by choosing the spectral approximation and then $b$.

Choose a countable $L^2$-dense subset $\{x_j:j\geq1\}$ of the self-adjoint unit ball of $M$, and enumerate all requirements $(j,k)\in\mathbb N^2$. Begin with $F_0=\mathbb C1$. If the current stage is a unital full matrix algebra $F_n$, then $N_n=F_n'\cap M$ is a $\mathrm{II}_1$ factor: matrix units identify $M$ with a matrix algebra over a corner and $N_n$ with that corner. For the next requirement $(j,k)$, apply the approximation step in $N_n$ to $y=E_{N_n}(x_j)$, with error less than $1/k$, obtaining $F\subset N_n$. Set $F_{n+1}=F_n\vee F$. This is again a full matrix algebra, its size is at least twice that of $F_n$, and $N_{n+1}=F'\cap N_n$. The identity $E_{N_{n+1}}=E_{N_{n+1}}E_{N_n}$ gives $$\lVert E_{N_{n+1}}(x_j)-\tau(x_j)1\rVert_2<1/k.$$ All previously obtained bounds persist because the relative commutants decrease and their trace expectations compose.

Let $R=(\bigcup_nF_n)''$ and $N=R'\cap M=\bigcap_nN_n$. Since $E_N=E_NE_{N_n}$, every requirement remains valid with $N$ in place of $N_{n+1}$. Thus $E_N(x_j)=\tau(x_j)1$ for every $j$. Density gives $E_N(x)=\tau(x)1$ for all $x\in M$, so $N=\mathbb C1$. In particular $R$ is a factor. It is finite and contains full matrix algebras of unbounded size, hence is of type $\mathrm{II}_1$, with the asserted increasing-matrix presentation. ◻

</details>

For a finite tracial von Neumann algebra $P$ and a nonprincipal ultrafilter $\omega$ on $\mathbb N$, write $P^\omega=\ell^\infty(\mathbb N,P)/\mathcal I_\omega$, where $\mathcal I_\omega$ consists of the uniformly bounded sequences $(a_i)$ with $\lim_\omega\|a_i\|_2=0$. Its trace is $\tau_\omega([a_i])=\lim_\omega\tau(a_i)$, and $P$ embeds by constant sequences. The lemma below places a free subfactor in such an ultrapower while retaining its inclusion in the prescribed subalgebra $Q^\omega$.

<a id="stmt-lem-free-position"></a>
<div id="lem:free-position" class="lemma">

**Lemma 20** (Free position in the full ultrapower). *Let $Q\subset P$ be a unital irreducible inclusion of $\mathrm{II}_1$ factors, with $P$ of separable predual. For any nonprincipal ultrafilter $\omega$ on $\mathbb N$, there is a subfactor $D\subset Q^\omega$, generated by increasing unital full matrix algebras of unbounded sizes, which is free from the constant copy of $P$ in the tracial ultrapower $P^\omega$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-free-position"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-free-position</summary>

*Proof.* We use the ultraproduct-of-subalgebras case of Popa’s independence theorem (Popa 2014, Theorem 0.1(a); see also Theorem 4.3) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[Popa2014](#ref-Popa2014)]. For von Neumann subalgebras $A,B\subset M$, the notation $A\prec_M B$ means Popa’s intertwining of a nonzero corner of $A$ into a finite matrix amplification of $B$. Its hypotheses are finite factors $M_i$ with $\dim M_i\longrightarrow\infty$, and unital von Neumann subalgebras $Q_i\subset M_i$ satisfying $Q_i\nprec_{M_i}(Q_i'\cap M_i)$. Write $\mathbf M=\prod_\omega M_i$, $\mathbf Q=\prod_\omega Q_i$, and $C=\mathbf Q'\cap\mathbf M$. Given an $L^2$-separable subspace $X\subset\mathbf M\ominus C$, the theorem supplies a diffuse abelian von Neumann algebra $A\subset\mathbf Q$ such that $$E_C(x_0a_1x_1\cdots a_nx_n)=0$$ whenever $n\geq1$, $x_0\in X\cup\{1\}$, $x_i\in X$ for $1\leq i\leq n$, and $a_i\in A\ominus\mathbb C1$. In particular, if $C=\mathbb C1$ and $T\subset\mathbf M$ is a separable-predual von Neumann subalgebra, take $X=T\ominus\mathbb C1$. The moment identities, traciality, and decomposition into scalar and centered parts show that $A$ is free from $T$. Here separability is for $L^2$, as stipulated in Popa (2014, sec. 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[Popa2014](#ref-Popa2014)].

We check these hypotheses for the constant sequences $M_i=P$ and $Q_i=Q$. The dimension condition holds because these factors are infinite-dimensional. If $Q\prec_P\mathbb C1$, the intertwining data would include a nonzero normal $*$-homomorphism from a nonzero corner of $Q$ into a finite-dimensional matrix algebra. Such a homomorphism from a factor is faithful, whereas a $\mathrm{II}_1$ factor has arbitrarily large families of nonzero orthogonal projections. This is impossible. Since $Q'\cap P=\mathbb C1$, the non-intertwining hypothesis follows.

We also verify the full ultrapower relative commutant: $$(Q^\omega)'\cap P^\omega=\mathbb C1.$$ For a uniformly bounded representative $(x_i)$ of an element of this commutant, commuting with all sequences of unitaries from $Q$ implies $$d_i:=\sup_{v\in\mathcal U(Q)}
             \lVert vx_iv^*-x_i\rVert_2\longrightarrow_\omega0.$$ Otherwise one could choose a witnessing unitary at each coordinate on an $\omega$-large set. The $L^2$-closed convex hull of the $Q$-unitary orbit of $x_i$ has a unique vector of minimum norm. It is $Q$-central, has trace $\tau(x_i)$, and belongs to $P$: the hull stays in the operator-norm ball of radius $\lVert x_i\rVert$, which is $L^2$-closed by weak-\* compactness and trace pairings. Irreducibility makes this vector $\tau(x_i)1$. Every point in the hull is within $d_i$ of $x_i$, so $\lVert x_i-\tau(x_i)1\rVert_2\leq d_i$. Bounded scalar sequences equal their scalar ultralimit in the tracial ultrapower. This proves the claim, which concerns all of $Q^\omega$, rather than merely the constant copy of $Q$.

Choose a Haar unitary $u_1$ in the diffuse abelian algebra obtained by applying Popa’s theorem to the constant $P$. Then $u_1\in Q^\omega$ is free from $P$. The algebra $W^*(P,u_1)$ has separable $L^2$ space: countably many words in generators of $P$, in $u_1$, and in their adjoints have linear span dense in $L^2$. A second application of the same theorem therefore gives a Haar unitary $u_2\in Q^\omega$ free from $W^*(P,u_1)$. Expanding centered reduced words shows that $P,W^*(u_1),W^*(u_2)$ are jointly free. Thus $W^*(u_1,u_2)$ is free from $P$ and, by its free Haar moments, is trace-isomorphic to $L(\mathbb F_2)$. This is a separable-predual $\mathrm{II}_1$ factor: the nontrivial conjugacy classes of $\mathbb F_2$ are infinite, so a central square-summable Fourier coefficient function is supported at the identity. Apply Lemma <a href="#lem:irreducible-hyperfinite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:irreducible-hyperfinite">19</a> inside $W^*(u_1,u_2)$ to obtain $D$. Freeness passes to its subalgebra $D$, proving the lemma. The ambient tracial ultrapower throughout is $P^\omega$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-finite-copies"></a>
<div id="prop:finite-copies" class="proposition">

**Proposition 21** (Finite standard multiplicities). *Let $M$ be a separable-predual $\mathrm{II}_1$ factor, and let $M$ act on $H_r=L^2(M)^r$ by common left multiplication, where $r\geq1$ is finite. For every $Y\in\mathcal B(H_r)$, every finite $h\geq1$, and every $X\in M_h(M)$, $$\lVert[Y^{(h)},X]\rVert
       \leq (3c_1+2)\,g_M(Y)\,\lVert X\rVert.$$ Here $X$ denotes its entrywise action on $H_r^h$; the constant is independent of $M,r,h$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-finite-copies"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-finite-copies</summary>

*Proof.* Put $\delta=g_M(Y)$. By homogeneity it suffices to consider $\lVert X\rVert\leq1$. We first arrange a symmetry test on a square array of standard copies. Choose $h_0\geq h$ so that $m=2h_0\geq r$, extend $Y$ by zero to $H_m$, and retain the notation $Y$. The copy projection reduces the common action of $M$, so this extension still has scalar commutator bound $\delta$. For $X'=X\oplus0\in M_{h_0}(M)$, define in $P=M_m(M)$ $$s=
 \begin{pmatrix}
  (1-X'X'^*)^{1/2}&X'\\
  X'^*&-(1-X'^*X')^{1/2}
 \end{pmatrix}.$$ Functional calculus gives $(1-X'X'^*)^{1/2}X'=X'(1-X'^*X')^{1/2}$, and hence $s=s^*=s^{-1}$. Traciality gives equal moments for $X'X'^*$ and $X'^*X'$, including the zeroth moment; polynomial approximation on $[0,1]$ gives equal traces for their displayed square roots. Consequently $\tau_P(s)=0$.

Write vectors of $H_m^m$ as arrays $(\xi_{ij})$, with $i$ the outer amplification coordinate and $j$ the copy coordinate. The map $(\xi_{ij})\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}%
  \mapsto
  \EndAccSupp{}}\sqrt m\,(\xi_{ij})$ is a unitary from $H_m^m$ to $L^2(P)$, since $\lVert(\xi_{ij})\rVert_2^2=m^{-1}\sum_{i,j}\lVert\xi_{ij}\rVert_2^2$ on the latter space. Under this identification, the matrix action of $P$ is left multiplication. The upper-right block of $[Y^{(m)},L_s]$ is the commutator with $X'$. Restricting its row coordinates to the original $h$ and its copy coordinates to the original $r$ recovers $[Y^{(h)},X]$ for the original operator. It is therefore enough to bound $[Y^{(m)},L_s]$.

Choose $R\subset M$ from Lemma <a href="#lem:irreducible-hyperfinite" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:irreducible-hyperfinite">19</a>, with matrix stages $F_n$. On $H_m$, average $Y$ over the compact unitary group of each $F_n$, using common left multiplication, and take a weak operator cluster point $Y_0$ as $n$ tends to infinity. For every such unitary $v$, $\lVert L_vYL_v^*-Y\rVert\leq\delta$. The averages, and hence their cluster point, are within $\delta$ of $Y$. An average over a later stage commutes with every earlier stage, so $$[Y_0,L_R]=0,\qquad
 \lVert Y_0-Y\rVert\leq\delta,\qquad g_M(Y_0)\leq3\delta.$$ The first identity extends from the matrix stages to $R$ by strong density; the norm bounds pass to the cluster point by weak operator closedness of norm balls.

On $L^2(P)=H_m^m$, set $S=Y_0^{(m)}$ and $Q=M_m(R)$. Matrix units and $R'\cap M=\mathbb C1$ imply $Q'\cap P=\mathbb C1$. Moreover $S$ commutes with $L_Q$, since it has the same operator $Y_0$ on every row and $Y_0$ commutes with every entry from $R$. For $e_0=e_{11}\otimes1$, the space $e_0L^2(P)$ is one row of $m$ standard copies, with $e_0Pe_0\cong M$ acting by common left multiplication. Since $S$ commutes with $L_{e_0}$, it follows on the full Hilbert space that $$\lVert[S,L_z]\rVert\leq3\delta\lVert z\rVert\qquad(z\in e_0Pe_0).$$ If $f\in Q$ is a projection with $\tau_P(f)\leq1/m$, projection comparison in the factor $Q$ gives a unitary $v\in Q$ with $f\leq ve_0v^*$. Conjugating by $L_v$, which commutes with $S$, yields $$\lVert[S,L_z]\rVert\leq3\delta\lVert z\rVert
       \qquad(z\in fPf,\ f\in Q,\ \tau_P(f)\leq1/m).$$

We have obtained the same small-corner estimate for every projection of $Q$ of trace at most $1/m$. The next step transports that estimate to the ultrapower, where Lemma <a href="#lem:free-position" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:free-position">20</a> supplies the free subfactor. Fix a nonprincipal ultrafilter $\omega$ on $\mathbb N$. Recall the tracial quotient and distinguish it from the Hilbert ultrapower: $$\begin{aligned}
 P^\omega&=\ell^\infty(\mathbb N,P)/\mathcal I_\omega,
 &\mathcal I_\omega&=
 \{(a_i):\lim_\omega\lVert a_i\rVert_2=0\},\\
 \mathcal H&=(L^2(P))^\omega_{\mathrm{Hilb}}.
 \end{aligned}$$ The Hilbert ultrapower uses bounded Hilbert-space sequences modulo those whose Hilbert norms tend to zero along $\omega$. Sending the class of a uniformly operator-bounded sequence $(a_i)$ to its Hilbert-ultrapower class is a trace-preserving isometry on the dense subspace $P^\omega\subset L^2(P^\omega)$. Its extension identifies $\mathcal C=L^2(P^\omega)$ with a closed subspace of $\mathcal H$.

For a bounded sequence $a=(a_i)$ in $P$, its coordinatewise left action on $\mathcal H$, and the adjoint action corresponding to $(a_i^*)$, preserve the bounded-algebra-sequence subspace of $\mathcal C$. Thus $\mathcal C$ reduces both actions. If $a\in\mathcal I_\omega$, then for every operator-bounded sequence $(b_i)$, $$\lVert a_ib_i\rVert_2\leq\lVert b_i\rVert\lVert a_i\rVert_2
                       \longrightarrow_\omega0.$$ It follows by density that the action on $\mathcal C$ factors through the tracial quotient and is precisely left multiplication by $[a_i]$. This factoring assertion is needed only on $\mathcal C$, not on the whole Hilbert ultrapower.

Let $S^{\omega}_{\mathrm{Hilb}}$ be the coordinatewise action of the fixed bounded operator $S$ on $\mathcal H$, and set $$\widehat S=P_{\mathcal C}S^{\omega}_{\mathrm{Hilb}}
                              \big|_{\mathcal C}.$$ Since $S$ commutes with every left multiplier from $Q$, its Hilbert ultrapower commutes with every bounded left $Q$-sequence. Compression to the reducing subspace $\mathcal C$ proves $[\widehat S,L_{Q^\omega}]=0$.

<a id="eq:ultrapower-corner"></a>
Set $t=1/(2m)$. We claim that $$\label{eq:ultrapower-corner}
 \begin{gathered}
 \lVert[\widehat S,L_z]\rVert\leq3\delta\lVert z\rVert,\\
 e\in Q^\omega\text{ a projection},\qquad
       \tau(e)=t,\qquad z\in eP^\omega e.
 \end{gathered}$$ To lift $e$, take a bounded self-adjoint representative $(a_i)$ in $Q$ and set $f_i=1_{[1/2,\infty)}(a_i)$. The scalar inequality $\lvert x-1_{[1/2,\infty)}(x)\rvert\leq2\lvert x^2-x\rvert$ gives $\lVert f_i-a_i\rVert_2\longrightarrow_\omega0$, so $[f_i]=e$ and $\tau(f_i)\longrightarrow_\omega t$. The strict gap $t<1/m$ allows us to replace $f_i$ by zero off an $\omega$-large set and assume $\tau(f_i)\leq1/m$ for every $i$.

Given $z\in eP^\omega e$ and $\eta>0$, the $C^*$-quotient norm gives a representative $(b_i)$ with $\sup_i\lVert b_i\rVert\leq\lVert z\rVert+\eta$. The sequence $z_i=f_ib_if_i$ represents $z$, lies in the required corners, and has the same norm bound. Consequently $$\lVert[S,L_{z_i}]\rVert\leq3\delta(\lVert z\rVert+\eta)
 \qquad\text{for every }i.$$ This estimate passes to the Hilbert ultrapower. Its compression to $\mathcal C$ is $[\widehat S,L_z]$, since $\mathcal C$ reduces the bounded left sequence action. Letting $\eta$ decrease to zero proves <a href="#eq:ultrapower-corner" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ultrapower-corner">[eq:ultrapower-corner]</a>.

By Lemma <a href="#lem:free-position" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:free-position">20</a>, choose $D\subset Q^\omega$ free from the constant $P$, and put $\mathcal L=W^*(D,P)\subset P^\omega$. Faithfulness of the trace and the reduced-word inner products identify this algebra with the tracial free product $D*P$. Let $G=P_{L^2(\mathcal L)}\widehat S|_{L^2(\mathcal L)}$. The subspace $L^2(\mathcal L)$ reduces left multiplication by $\mathcal L$. Thus $G$ commutes with $L_D$, and <a href="#eq:ultrapower-corner" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ultrapower-corner">[eq:ultrapower-corner]</a> gives the corner hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:free-corner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:free-corner">9</a> with $A_0=P$, $\epsilon=3\delta$, and $t=1/(2m)$.

Finally, every constant $L^2(P)$-vector belongs to $\mathcal C$, by approximation by bounded vectors from $P$, and $S^{\omega}_{\mathrm{Hilb}}$ sends the constant vector $\xi$ to the constant vector $S\xi$. Both of these vectors also belong to $L^2(\mathcal L)$. Therefore both compressions preserve the action of $S$ on the constant subspace. As $L_s$ preserves that subspace, for $\xi,\eta\in L^2(P)$ we have exactly $$\langle[G,L_s]\xi,\eta\rangle
        =\langle[S,L_s]\xi,\eta\rangle.$$ Theorem <a href="#thm:free-corner" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:free-corner">9</a>, applied to the trace-zero symmetry $s\in P$, consequently yields on the original $L^2(P)$ $$\lVert[S,L_s]\rVert\leq3c_1\delta.$$ Replacing $Y_0$ by $Y$ costs at most $2\lVert Y_0-Y\rVert\lVert s\rVert\leq2\delta$, since Hilbert amplification preserves the difference norm. The initial block compression now gives the claimed bound $(3c_1+2)\delta$ for the original finite $r,h$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:global"></a>
### From finite factors to arbitrary homomorphisms

We now remove the restrictions on the algebra and its representation, and then pass from inner commutators to arbitrary derivations. Throughout this section set <a id="eq:global-constants"></a>$$\label{eq:global-constants}
 C_{\mathrm{fac}}
   =\max\{3c_1+2,\,1+2c_{\mathrm{row}},\,2\},
 \qquad C=3C_{\mathrm{fac}}+2.$$ These constants depend only on the numerical constants of the preceding sections.

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
#### Representations of factors

<a id="stmt-prop-factor-representations"></a>
<div id="prop:factor-representations" class="proposition">

**Proposition 22**. *Let $P\subset\mathcal B(K)$ be a unital factor acting on a separable Hilbert space. For every $Y\in\mathcal B(K)$, every $h\ge1$, and every $X\in M_h(P)$, $$\lVert[Y^{(h)},X]\rVert
   \le C_{\mathrm{fac}}\,g_P(Y)\,\lVert X\rVert.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-factor-representations"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-factor-representations</summary>

*Proof.* We first treat a type $\mathrm{II}_1$ factor $M$ in an arbitrary normal representation on a separable Hilbert space. Its predual is separable. Every cyclic normal representation embeds into the tracial standard representation. Indeed, if $\xi$ is a cyclic vector, its normal positive functional has a trace density $a\in L^1(M)_+$ (Hiai 2020, Corollary 5.14 and Remark 5.15) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[Hiai2020](#ref-Hiai2020)]. The map on cyclic orbits $$x\xi\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=27FC}%
  \begingroup
    \let\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}%
  \mapsto
  \EndAccSupp{}}\mapsto
    \let\longrightarrow\longrightarrow
    \longmapsto
  \endgroup
  \EndAccSupp{}}xa^{1/2}\in L^2(M)$$ is isometric, because both squared norms equal the functional evaluated at $x^*x$. Its extension intertwines the representations and has a reducing range. Decomposing into orthogonal cyclic reducing subspaces, of which there are at most countably many, embeds the given representation into $\widetilde K=L^2(M)^{\oplus\mathbb N}$. These are standard consequences of the normal representation theory of finite von Neumann algebras; see Takesaki (1979) <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[Takesaki1979](#ref-Takesaki1979)].

Extend $Y$ by zero off the embedded reducing subspace. The scalar commutator bound is unchanged. On the countable amplification, let $p_n$ be the projection onto the first $n$ copies. Since $p_n$ commutes with the common left action, $$[p_nYp_n,L_x]=p_n[Y,L_x]p_n\qquad(x\in M).$$ Proposition <a href="#prop:finite-copies" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:finite-copies">21</a> applies to the compression of $Y$ to $p_n\widetilde K$, with constant $3c_1+2$. For any fixed finite matrix test, the corresponding compressed commutators converge strongly to the full commutator as $n\to\infty$. A uniform operator-norm bound passes to this strong limit. Restricting back to the original reducing subspace proves the assertion for type $\mathrm{II}_1$ factors.

If $P$ is properly infinite, choose isometries $v_1,\ldots,v_h\in P$ with orthogonal ranges. The row $V=[v_1\ \cdots\ v_h]$ satisfies $V^*V=I_{K^h}$. Apply the row and column derivation estimates of Section <a href="#sec:preliminaries" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:preliminaries">2</a> to $x\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}%
  \mapsto
  \EndAccSupp{}}[Y,x]$. With $\delta=g_P(Y)$, they give $$\lVert YV-VY^{(h)}\rVert\le c_{\mathrm{row}}\delta,
 \qquad
 \lVert Y^{(h)}V^*-V^*Y\rVert\le c_{\mathrm{row}}\delta.$$ For $X\in M_h(P)$, the identity $$\begin{aligned}
&=(YV-VY^{(h)})XV^*
    +V[Y^{(h)},X]V^*\\
  &\hspace{2em}+VX(Y^{(h)}V^*-V^*Y)
\end{aligned}$$ and $\lVert VXV^*\rVert=\lVert X\rVert$ imply $$\lVert[Y^{(h)},X]\rVert
 \le(1+2c_{\mathrm{row}})\delta\lVert X\rVert.$$ Here conjugation by the isometry $V:K^h\to K$ preserves the norm of the middle term, regardless of whether its range is all of $K$.

Finally, if $P$ is a finite type I factor, average $Y$ over its compact unitary group to obtain $Y_0\in P'$ with $\lVert Y-Y_0\rVert\le\delta$. Then $$[Y^{(h)},X]=[(Y-Y_0)^{(h)},X],$$ which gives the constant $2$. These three cases exhaust the factors and prove the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Central decomposition

<a id="stmt-prop-separable-estimate"></a>
<div id="prop:separable-estimate" class="proposition">

**Proposition 23**. *Let $P\subset\mathcal B(K)$ be any unital von Neumann algebra on a separable Hilbert space. Then $$\lVert[Y^{(h)},X]\rVert\le C\,g_P(Y)\,\lVert X\rVert
 \qquad(Y\in\mathcal B(K),\ X\in M_h(P),\ h\ge1).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-separable-estimate"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-separable-estimate</summary>

<a id="eq:center-average"></a>
*Proof.* Write $\delta=g_P(Y)$. Average $Y$ over the compact unitary groups of the finite-dimensional algebras generated by finite families of central projections. Direct these families by inclusion and take a cofinal weak operator cluster limit. The resulting operator $Y_1$ commutes with every central projection, hence with $Z(P)$, and satisfies $$\label{eq:center-average}
 \lVert Y_1-Y\rVert\le\delta,
 \qquad g_P(Y_1)\le3\delta.$$ For the first inequality, each unitary being averaged belongs to $P$, so its conjugation changes $Y$ by at most $\delta$. The inequality is preserved by averages and weak operator limits. The second follows from $g_P(Y_1)\le g_P(Y)+2\lVert Y_1-Y\rVert$.

Use the central direct-integral decomposition on a separable Hilbert space (Takesaki 1979) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[Takesaki1979](#ref-Takesaki1979)]; precise section locators for the factor fibers, decomposable operators, and norm formula are Blackadar (2006, secs. III.1.6.1–4 and III.5.1.10–15) <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[Blackadar2006](#ref-Blackadar2006)]:

<a id="eq:fiber-commutator"></a>
$$K=\int^{\oplus}K_\alpha\,d\mu(\alpha),
 \qquad
 P=\int^{\oplus}P_\alpha\,d\mu(\alpha),$$ where the $P_\alpha$ are factors almost everywhere and the fibers are separable. The center acts as the scalar diagonal algebra, so its commutant consists of the decomposable operators. In particular, $Y_1$ has fibers $Y_1(\alpha)$. We verify carefully that $$\label{eq:fiber-commutator}
 g_{P_\alpha}\bigl(Y_1(\alpha)\bigr)\le3\delta
 \quad\text{for almost every }\alpha.$$

The separable direct-integral decomposition admits countably many bounded measurable sections whose values generate $P_\alpha$ almost everywhere; fix such a family. Take all rational complex $*$-polynomials in these sections, including the rational scalar constants. For each such section $b\in P$, form the contraction $$c_b=b(1+b^*b)^{-1/2}\in P.$$ On every fiber where the generators have the stated property, these contractions are norm dense in the contraction ball of the generated $C^*$-algebra. To see this, the polynomials are norm dense, the displayed transformation is norm continuous, and its range contains the open unit ball: its inverse there is $$c\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=27FC}%
  \begingroup
    \let\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}%
  \mapsto
  \EndAccSupp{}}\mapsto
    \let\longrightarrow\longrightarrow
    \longmapsto
  \endgroup
  \EndAccSupp{}}c(1-c^*c)^{-1/2}.$$ The closed unit ball is the norm closure of the open one. Kaplansky density then makes these countably many fiber contractions strongly dense in the unit ball of $P_\alpha$.

For each $c_b$, the bound $\lVert[Y_1,c_b]\rVert\le3\delta$ implies the same fiber bound outside a null set, by the essential-supremum norm formula. Removing the union of these countably many null sets leaves a single conull set on which every test holds. Strong approximation by the contraction tests passes the bound to every element of the fiber unit ball, proving <a href="#eq:fiber-commutator" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:fiber-commutator">[eq:fiber-commutator]</a>.

Apply Proposition <a href="#prop:factor-representations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:factor-representations">22</a> on that conull set. For any fixed $X\in M_h(P)$, decomposability of its entries and the same norm formula give $$\lVert[Y_1^{(h)},X]\rVert
 \le3C_{\mathrm{fac}}\delta\lVert X\rVert.$$ Restoring $Y$ costs at most $2\delta\lVert X\rVert$ by <a href="#eq:center-average" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:center-average">[eq:center-average]</a>, giving $C=3C_{\mathrm{fac}}+2$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Removing separability

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-separable-estimate-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-separable-estimate</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:universal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:universal">2</a>.* Let $P\subset\mathcal B(K)$ be arbitrary, and fix $h\ge1$, $X\in M_h(P)$, and a unit vector $\xi=(\xi_1,\ldots,\xi_h)\in K^h$. Let $A_X\subset P$ be the unital $C^*$-algebra generated by the entries of $X$. The $C^*$-algebra in $\mathcal B(K)$ generated by these entries and $Y$ is norm separable. The closed span of its orbits of the finitely many $\xi_i$ is therefore a separable common reducing subspace $K_0$ for $A_X$ and $Y$. Denote restriction of $A_X$ to $K_0$ by $\sigma$, put $Y_0=Y|_{K_0}$, and set $P_0=\sigma(A_X)''$.

For $a\in A_X$, $$\lVert[Y_0,\sigma(a)]\rVert\le g_P(Y)\lVert a\rVert.$$ The $C^*$-quotient norm of the image gives, for every $b\in\sigma(A_X)$, $$\lVert[Y_0,b]\rVert
 \le g_P(Y)\inf_{\sigma(a)=b}\lVert a\rVert
 =g_P(Y)\lVert b\rVert.$$ By Kaplansky density the same scalar commutator bound holds on $P_0$. Proposition <a href="#prop:separable-estimate" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:separable-estimate">23</a>, applied to $P_0$ and $\sigma_h(X)$, now yields $$\lVert[Y_0^{(h)},\sigma_h(X)]\rVert
 \le C g_P(Y)\lVert\sigma_h(X)\rVert
 \le C g_P(Y)\lVert X\rVert.$$ Since $K_0$ reduces all the operators in this test and contains every component of $\xi$, the restricted commutator on $\xi$ equals the original one. Taking the supremum over unit test vectors proves <a href="#eq:universal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:universal">[eq:universal]</a>. If $K=\{0\}$, the estimate is vacuous. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Derivations and similarity

Theorem <a href="#thm:universal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:universal">2</a> now controls inner commutators in every representation. To reach Kirchberg’s criterion we must implement an arbitrary bounded derivation. Cyclic-domain implementation supplies finitely many columns at a time; the uniform commutator estimate lets us pass from these finite collections to the full derivation.

<a id="stmt-prop-all-derivations"></a>
<div id="prop:all-derivations" class="proposition">

**Proposition 24**. *Let $A$ be a unital $C^*$-algebra and $\sigma:A\to\mathcal B(K)$ a unital $*$-representation. Every bounded $\sigma$-derivation $\Delta:A\to\mathcal B(K)$ satisfies $$\lVert\Delta\rVert_{\mathrm{cb}}\le C\lVert\Delta\rVert$$ and is implemented by a bounded operator on $K$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-all-derivations"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-all-derivations</summary>

*Proof.* Decompose $K$ into cyclic reducing subspaces for $\sigma$: $$K=\bigoplus_{i\in I}K_i.$$ Such a decomposition is obtained by taking a maximal orthogonal family of cyclic reducing subspaces; a nonzero vector in its orthogonal complement would generate another one. The index set $I$ can have any cardinality. Let $q_i$ denote the projection onto $K_i$.

<a id="eq:finite-column-identity"></a>
The map $a\mathrel{%
  \BeginAccSupp{method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}%
  \mapsto
  \EndAccSupp{}}\Delta(a)|_{K_i}$ is a rectangular derivation with cyclic domain representation $\sigma|_{K_i}$ and full output representation $\sigma$. Lemma <a href="#lem:cyclic-implementation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cyclic-implementation">7</a> gives a bounded map $V_i:K_i\to K$ such that $$\Delta(a)|_{K_i}=V_i\sigma(a)|_{K_i}-\sigma(a)V_i.$$ For a finite subset $F\subset I$, put $$q_F=\sum_{i\in F}q_i,
 \qquad V_F=\sum_{i\in F}V_iq_i\in\mathcal B(K).$$ Every $V_F$ is bounded, and the reducing property gives the exact identity $$\label{eq:finite-column-identity}
 [V_F,\sigma(a)]=\Delta(a)q_F\qquad(a\in A).$$ Only the scalar commutator norm, rather than the norm of $V_F$, needs to be uniform in $F$. Indeed, taking the infimum over lifts of an element $b\in\sigma(A)$ in <a href="#eq:finite-column-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:finite-column-identity">[eq:finite-column-identity]</a> gives $$\lVert[V_F,b]\rVert\le\lVert\Delta\rVert\lVert b\rVert.$$ This uses the $C^*$-quotient norm of the $*$-image and does not require $\sigma$ to be faithful. Kaplansky density then implies $$g_{\sigma(A)''}(V_F)\le\lVert\Delta\rVert.$$

Apply Theorem <a href="#thm:universal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:universal">2</a> to $V_F$ and $\sigma(A)''$. For every $X\in M_h(A)$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \lVert\Delta_h(X)q_F^{(h)}\rVert
   &=\lVert[V_F^{(h)},\sigma_h(X)]\rVert\\
   &\le C\lVert\Delta\rVert\lVert\sigma_h(X)\rVert
    \le C\lVert\Delta\rVert\lVert X\rVert.
\end{aligned}$$ The projections $q_F$ converge strongly to the identity as a net indexed by finite subsets of $I$. Hence the displayed bound passes to $\Delta_h(X)$ for every fixed $h$ and $X$. This proves the claimed complete boundedness. Lemma <a href="#lem:cb-implementation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:cb-implementation">5</a> then supplies a bounded implementing operator. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-all-derivations-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-all-derivations</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:similarity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:similarity">1</a>.* For every faithful unital $*$-representation of $A$, each bounded derivation into the ambient bounded operators is inner by Proposition <a href="#prop:all-derivations" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:all-derivations">24</a>. Thus condition (2) of Theorem <a href="#thm:kirchberg" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:kirchberg">8</a> holds for $A$. Its equivalent similarity property gives, for every bounded unital homomorphism $\pi:A\to\mathcal B(H)$, a bounded invertible $S$ with bounded inverse such that $S\pi(\cdot)S^{-1}$ is a $*$-homomorphism. The identity isomorphism treats the zero Hilbert space as well. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:hyperreflexivity"></a>
#### Hyperreflexivity

The uniform commutator estimate also controls distance to any von Neumann algebra. We apply it to the commutant, whose projections describe the algebra’s invariant subspaces.

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-all-derivations-3"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-all-derivations</summary>

*Proof of Corollary <a href="#cor:hyperreflexivity" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:hyperreflexivity">3</a>.* Put $P=M'$. Since $M$ is self-adjoint, its invariant subspaces are reducing and their orthogonal projections are exactly the projections in $P$. Hence $\alpha_M$ is the usual off-diagonal distance seminorm. For every projection $e\in P$, the two off-diagonal blocks give $$\|[T,e]\|=\max\{\|(1-e)Te\|,\|eT(1-e)\|\}\le\alpha_M(T),$$ where the last inequality also uses the projection $1-e$. Spectral step approximations of a self-adjoint contraction $a\in P$ express it, modulo a scalar, as a norm limit of linear combinations of projections in $P$ whose coefficients have total absolute value at most two. Thus $\|[T,a]\|\le2\alpha_M(T)$. The real and imaginary parts of any contraction in $P$ are self-adjoint contractions, so $g_P(T)\le4\alpha_M(T)$.

Arveson’s commutant-distance formula, in the form recalled in Christensen et al. (2010, Equation (2.1)) <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[ChristensenSinclairSmithWhite2010](#ref-ChristensenSinclairSmithWhite2010)], gives $$2\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(T,M)=\|\operatorname{ad}_T|_P\|_{\mathrm{cb}},
 \qquad \operatorname{ad}_T(a)=[T,a],$$ since $P'=M$. Taking the supremum over finite matrix sizes in Theorem <a href="#thm:universal" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:universal">2</a> therefore yields $$2\mathop{\mathrm{dist}}(T,M)\le Cg_P(T)\le4C\alpha_M(T),$$ which proves Equation <a href="#eq:universal-hyperreflexivity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:universal-hyperreflexivity">[eq:universal-hyperreflexivity]</a>. ◻

</details>

{
  "edges": [
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "symmetric",
      "explanation": "Both results address bounded changes of Hilbert space coordinates that make representations preserve the relevant adjoint or unitary structure. Their algebra and group hypotheses differ.",
      "relation": "shared_technique",
      "source_anchor": "stmt-thm-similarity",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-288.1",
      "source_quote": "Let $A$ be any unital complex $C^*$-algebra, let $H$ be any complex Hilbert space, and let $\\pi:A\\to\\mathcal B(H)$ be a bounded, complex-linear, unital algebra homomorphism. There exists an operator $S\\in\\mathcal B(H)$, invertible with bounded inverse, such that $$\\rho(a)=S\\pi(a)S^{-1}\\qquad(a\\in A)$$ is a $*$-homomorphism. In particular, $\\rho(a^*)=\\rho(a)^*$ for every $a\\in A$.",
      "target_anchor": "stmt-thm-main",
      "target_pm_id": "PM-251.1",
      "target_quote": "A discrete group is amenable if and only if it is unitarizable.",
      "verification": "PENDING"
    }
  ],
